Let me carefully analyze this massive codebase and audit request. I need to find real defects in five Solana programs (SEAL, MINT, BURN, GOV, SWEEP) and the off-chain pipeline. Let me work through the targets systematically.

Let me start by understanding the architecture and then looking for specific vulnerabilities.

Key programs:
1. SEAL (`programs/cbob-vaults/src/lib.rs`) - seal/thaw with burn pairing
2. MINT (`programs/cbob-vault-mint/src/lib.rs`) - mint, charter claim, level draw, pool, boosts, governance actions


3. BURN (`programs/cbob-vault-burn/src/lib.rs`) - burn with emptiness proof, rate limit
4. GOV (`programs/cbob-vault-gov/src/lib.rs`) - governance
5. SWEEP (`programs/cbob-vault-sweep/src/lib.rs`) - stateless sweep helper

Now I'm diving into the critical areas to find actual bugs. Let me examine the sweep helper's access control first—it's stateless and relies on Core's ExecuteV1 to authorize operations, with the asset signer PDA signing as the vault's asset signer.

Looking at the sweep logic, it validates that the signer is actually signing, checks the token and system program IDs, prevents self-transfers, then iterates through token pairs to verify ownership and mint matching before transferring and closing accounts. Finally it sweeps all lamports from the signer to the destination.

Now I'm examining the lamport sweep more carefully—specifically whether the system transfer can actually empty a PDA to zero. The asset-signer PDA is a system account with no data, so it should be able to be emptied completely. But I need to think through what happens when a system account reaches zero lamports...

Actually, the key insight is that the sweep happens before the burn in the same transaction. The sweep empties the PDA to zero, then BURN checks that lamports equals zero and proceeds with the burn. Since this is all atomic within one transaction, the PDA stays at zero when BURN validates it. The question is whether anything could add lamports back to the PDA between the sweep and the burn check.

Looking at the instruction sequence: claim, open, sweep, burn. The sweep empties the PDA, and nothing in the burn instruction itself adds lamports back. So the emptiness check should pass. But I'm wondering if there's a way to deposit lamports into the PDA after the sweep but before the burn within the same transaction—though that would require an instruction between them, which the pairing logic prevents.

Now I'm examining the BURN emptiness proof more carefully. It checks that the asset signer has zero lamports and validates all reserve token accounts...

Checking reserve token accounts... I'm noticing a potential issue with how the code handles token accounts that have extensions. When an account is owned by SPL_TOKEN_2022_ID and has extensions, the length check `d.len() >= TOKEN_ACCOUNT_LEN` passes, but then the code reads the owner field at offset 32-64 and compares it to the PDA. If the owner matches, it proceeds to check for delegates and other fields, but the logic seems incomplete or potentially problematic for accounts with extensions. I see the issue now—when a Token-2022 account has extensions, the code reads the owner field at the standard offset and compares it to the PDA, but if that matches, it then rejects the account for having extensions. The problem is that a Token-2022 account with extensions could have a different data layout where the owner field isn't at the expected offset, potentially causing the check to fail or pass incorrectly depending on how the extensions are structured. The reserve loop handles this correctly by checking the owner field first and skipping if it doesn't match the PDA, then validating the account length. But the second loop has a flaw: it reads the owner field at a fixed offset without verifying the account is actually a token account first, so a Token-2022 account with extensions could have garbage at that offset that happens to match the PDA, causing it to incorrectly reject the account.

Now I'm looking at the rate limit logic in BURN...

Examining rate limit logic... I'm verifying the ring buffer indexing works correctly—when the head wraps around, the k-th most recent entry is still accessible at the right position. Now I'm looking at the SEAL and BURN pairing logic to see how they validate each other's instructions.

SEAL checks for a matching burn instruction after the current one by scanning forward through the instruction list, while BURN does the reverse—looking backward for a matching open instruction. Both use the same account indices to bind the asset and owner together. The key question is whether an attacker could exploit this by placing a burn instruction for a different asset in the same transaction to satisfy SEAL's check.

Looking at the account index validation, both programs verify that account 0 matches the asset and account 2 matches the owner, so a burn for a different asset would fail these checks. But I'm wondering if there's a way to construct a burn instruction with the correct asset and owner at those positions while still doing something malicious with the remaining accounts.

Actually, the burn instruction itself validates the asset owner and collection, so that's protected. Now I'm looking at the MINT pool accounting to see if there's a vulnerability in how `owed_scaled` is tracked and whether the pool always maintains sufficient balance.

The `credit` function distributes available funds by calculating a per-weight-unit amount and updating both the accumulator and total owed. The `claim` function then pays out each vault's share based on their weight and accumulated debt, with the pool balance checked against the ceiling of owed amounts.

Now I'm tracing through the `owed_of` calculation to understand how carry and debt interact—it looks like carry gets added before subtracting debt, which matters after a weight change resets the debt to the new weight times the accumulator.

When `reweigh` is called, it captures the current owed amount into carry, then resets debt to the new weight times the accumulator. This means the owed calculation becomes `new_weight * acc + old_owed - new_weight * acc`, which simplifies back to `old_owed`—so the vault's owed amount stays consistent through the weight change.

Now I'm looking at the boost ring logic to see if there's an issue with how boosts are scheduled and expired. The ring tracks how many boosts end at each epoch modulo 64, and when a boost is created, it increments the count at the slot corresponding to its end epoch. When advancing epochs, the code moves boosts from the current slot into the due count and clears that slot.

The key insight is that boosts lasting at most 63 distributions can't collide in the same ring slot since 63 < 64. But I'm noticing a potential issue: when a boost is purchased, its end epoch is calculated as the current epoch plus the boost duration, and it gets scheduled into the ring at that future slot. However, the boost becomes active immediately and earns from the very next distribution, which happens when the epoch advances. So there's a timing question about whether the boost is earning distributions before it's actually scheduled to expire.

Let me trace through the logic more carefully. When `boost_vault` is called, the end epoch is set to `current_epoch + epochs`, and the ring slot is marked for that end epoch. Then when `advance_epoch` runs, it increments the epoch and checks if any boosts are expiring at the new epoch. The question is whether a boost with `epochs = 1` actually earns exactly one distribution or if there's an off-by-one error in how the expiration is scheduled. I'm working through the timing of when boosts expire relative to distributions. The key insight is that `boost_end_epoch` marks when a boost becomes due and stops earning, so a boost bought at epoch e with N epochs earns exactly N distributions—from e+1 through e+N—and then expires at e+N+1. Let me verify this holds across the test cases to make sure the logic is consistent. I'm verifying the boost expiration logic across different scenarios. When a boost is bought at epoch e with N=2, it earns at e+1 and e+2, then becomes due at e+3 when the epoch advances past its end. The test confirms this: after one more distribution it's due, and after N more distributions from the purchase point it's also due. The pattern holds consistently.

Now I'm looking at the governance quorum calculation...

Checking quorum logic... The math checks out—10.71% YES votes satisfies both the 10% turnout requirement and the 51% threshold of votes cast. Now I'm looking at the key change mechanism, which requires half of all live vaults to approve. The snapshot of live supply is taken when the proposal is created, so vaults minted afterward can't vote or affect the threshold. But I'm wondering if there's a way to manipulate this by burning vaults after the snapshot is taken.

If someone burns vaults after the snapshot, the denominator stays fixed at the original count, so the requirement remains half of that snapshot. This actually makes it harder to pass since there are fewer vaults available to vote. The real question is whether someone could game the snapshot itself by creating and burning vaults before the proposal is created to manipulate what counts as "live supply."

Looking at the math: if an attacker mints and burns vaults before the snapshot, they're just reducing the denominator, which makes it easier for their remaining vaults to reach the 50% threshold. But they'd need to actually own enough vaults to hit that threshold anyway. The charter window limits how many vaults can be created initially, so there's a natural cap on this attack vector.

Now I'm examining the sweep helper's token pair validation logic...

Validating token pair ownership... I see the issue now—when a source appears twice in the pairs list, the first iteration transfers and closes it, but then the second iteration tries to read from an already-closed account. The `read_token` function checks if the account is still owned by the token program, and since closing an account sets its owner to the system program, that check fails and returns an error. So the duplicate source case is actually caught, just indirectly through the account closure rather than an explicit duplicate check.

Now I'm looking at the lamport sweep logic...

The sweep transfers all lamports from the signer to the destination, but there's a potential issue if the destination is a token account owned by the SPL Token program rather than a system account. The system transfer instruction might fail or behave unexpectedly when the recipient isn't a system-owned account.

Looking at the test case R27.S1, it seems the transfer actually succeeds and credits lamports to a non-System-owned destination while preserving the token data. This suggests the runtime allows lamport transfers to any account regardless of owner, as long as the source is a system account with sufficient funds. The key constraint is that the source must be a system account (which the asset-signer PDA is), and the transfer itself doesn't validate the destination's owner.

Now I'm looking at the `monthly_inline` function to see if there are any issues with how it handles the monthly distribution logic.

If there are available funds and active weight, it credits the distribution.
